PREFACE

This Report has been prepared for submission to the Governor under
Article 151 of the Constitution.

Chapter 1 of this report indicates auditee profile, authority for audit,
planning and conduct of audit, organizational structure of office of the
Principal Accountant General (Civil Audit) and response of the
Departments to the draft paragraphs. Highlights of audit observations
included in this report have also been brought out in this Chapter.

Chapter 2 deals with the findings of performance audit while
Chapter 3 covers audit of transactions in various departments
including autonomous bodies. Chapter 4 comments on functioning of
a Government Department.

The cases mentioned in this Report are among those which came to
notice in the course of test audit of accounts during the year 2009-10 as
well as those which had come to notice in earlier years but could not be
dealt with in previous Reports; matters relating to the period
subsequent to 2009-10 have also been included, wherever necessary.

vii



Chapter 1

Introduction

1.1 About this Report

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (C&AG) relates
to matters arising from compliance audit of the transactions of the Civil and
Works departments of the Government of Uttar Pradesh, Centrally sponsored
and State plan schemes and audit of autonomous bodies of the State, including
performance audit of selected schemes and departments.

The primary purpose of the Report is to bring to the notice of the State
Legislature, important results of audit. Auditing standards require that the
materiality level of reporting should be commensurate with the nature,
volume and magnitude of transactions. The findings of audit are expected to
enable the Executive to take the corrective action as also to frame policies and
directives that lead to improved financial management of the organizations,
thus contributing to better governance.

Compliance audit refers to examination of the transactions relating to
expenditure, receipts, assets and liabilities of the audited entities to ascertain
whether the provision of the Constitution of India, applicable laws, rules and
regulations and various orders and instructions issued by the competent
authorities are being complied with.

Performance audit is an independent assessment or examination of the extent
to which an organisation, programme or scheme operates economically,
efficiently and effectively.

This Chapter provides the auditee profile, planning and extent of audit and
follow-up on audit reports. Chapter 2 of this Report deals with the findings of
performance audit and Chapter 3 deals with compliance audit in the various
departments and autonomous bodies. Chapter 4 contains results of CCO based
audit of Animal Husbandry Department.

The cases mentioned in the Report are those which came to notice in the
course of test audit of accounts during 2009-10 as well as those which had
come to light in earlier years but could not be dealt with in previous Reports.
Matters relating to the period subsequent to 2009-10 have also been included
wherever necessary.

1.2 Auditee profile

There are 72 departments at the Secretariat level, headed by Chief Secretary/
Principal Secretaries/Secretaries who are assisted by the Special Secretaries,
Deputy Secretaries and Directors and subordinates officers under them and 23
autonomous bodies which are under audit jurisdiction of the Principal
Accountant General (Civil Audit), Uttar Pradesh, Allahabad.

1.3. Authority for audit

The authority for audit by the C&AG is derived from the Articles 149 and 151
of the Constitution of India and the Comptroller and Auditor General's
(Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. Principal Accountant
General (Civil Audit) conducted audit of expenditure of civil and works
departments, autonomous bodies under sections 13, 14, 15, 19 and 20 of the
Comptroller & Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service)
Act, 1971. The principles and methodology for compliance audit are
prescribed in the manuals issued by the Comptroller & Auditor General.

1.4... Organisational structure of the office of the Principal Accountant
General (Civil Audit), Uttar Pradesh.

Under the directions of the C&AG, the Principal Accountant General (Civil
Audit), Uttar Pradesh, conducts audit of civil and works departments and
autonomous bodies through two groups each for inspection of the civil and
works departments, one group each for autonomous bodies and forest
department. During 2009-10, 71 audit parties conducted compliance audit of
the selected units under various civil and works departments of the State
Government, autonomous bodies, externally aided projects, etc.



Chapter 1: Introduction

1.5 Planning and conduct of audit

Audit process commences with the assessment of risk of various Government
departments/ organisations/ autonomous bodies and schemes/ projects, etc.,
based on expenditure, criticality’ complexity of activities, level of delegated
financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls and the concerns of
stakeholders. Previous audit findings are also considered in this exercise.

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports containing audit
findings are issued to the head of the unit. The units are requested to furnish
replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the Inspection
Report. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled or
further action for compliance is advised. The important audit observations
arising out of these Inspection Reports are processed for inclusion in the audit
reports.

During 2009-10, 15,800 party-days were used to carry out audit of 2,357 units
out of 6,931 units of various departments/ organisations. The audit plan
covered those units/entities which were vulnerable to significant risk, as per
the assessment.

1.6. Significant observations of Performance Audit

Performance audit is undertaken to ensure whether the Government
programmes have achieved the desired objectives at the minimum cost and
given the intended benefits. In the last few years, Audit has reported on
several significant deficiencies in implementation of various programmes/
activities through performance audits, as well as on the quality of internal
controls in selected departments, which impact on the success of programmes
and functioning of the departments.

This report contains results of performance audit of Implementation of Vidhan
Mandal Kshetriva Vikas Nidhi, Urban Health Services, Implementation of
State Road Fund scheme and e-Governance in Information Technology and
Electronics Department. Besides, department-centric audit of Animal
Husbandry Department was conducted. The focus has been on auditing the
specific programmes/schemes and offering suitable recommendations, with
the intention of providing an aid to the Executive in taking corrective action
and improving service delivery to the citizens. The salient features of the
performance audits are discussed below.

1.6.1 Implementation of Vidhan Mandal Kshetriya Vikas Nidhi

With a view to enabling the Legislators to address the locally felt needs of
their constituencies, the Government launched (1998-99) Vidhan Mandal
Kshetriya Vikas Nidhi. The performance audit of the Nidhi, covering the
period of 2005-10 revealed that huge amounts remained unspent at the close
of each financial year. The variations in the accounts remained un-reconciled.



Audit Report

for the year ended 31 March 2010

Substantial number of works did not relate to the locally felt needs.
Inadmissible/ irregular and non-durable projects were executed in violation of
the guidelines. Besides, not only a substantial number of works were lying
incomplete, a large number of projects spilled over.

(Paragraph 2.1)
1.6.2 Urban Heatth Services

In order to provide good quality specialized medical services to the people,
the Government decided to upgrade health facilities by establishing hospitals
and trauma centres in urban areas during the Eleventh Plan (2007-12).
Performance audit, covering the period of 2007-10 revealed that codal
provisions for preparation of budget were not followed. The plan of action for
procurement of equipment was not finalized delaying purchase of advanced
medical devices. There was substantial shortage of medical/ para medical staff
impacting adversely the department’s ability to cater to the medical care needs
of the people. None of the seven 100 bedded and two 300 bedded hospitals
were completed by the targeted date. Besides, construction of a children’s
hospital in Lucknow and TB cum-General Hospital at Gorakhpur was not
taken up due to non-availability of land.

(Paragraph 2.2)
1.6.3 Implementation of State Road Fund scheme

With a view to keeping the State roads free of potholes and patches by
continuously maintaining and repairing, the Government created (1998) the
State Road Fund and levied additional sales tax on diesel and petrol forming
the receipts of the Fund. Performance audit of the State Road Fund scheme,
covering the period 2005-10, revealed that the Public Works Department,
which was the nodal department, maintained the roads falling under its
jurisdiction only and did not consider the roads belonging to other
departments/bodies. Identification of roads for renewal and maintenance
without relevant data showed lack of transparency in their selection. Fifty-two
per cent to 59 per cent of roads were not maintained during the period of
review. There were also cases of surrender of funds and parking of funds in
PLA.

(Paragraph 2.3)
1.6.4 e-Governance in Uttar Pradesh

With a view to making all Government services accessible to the common
man through common service delivery outlets and ensuring efficiency and
transparency, etc. at affordable costs, the Government implemented (May
2006) Centrally sponsored ‘National e-Governance Programme’. Under the
programme, Information Technology core infrastructure like State Wide Area
Network, State Data Centre and Common Service Centres was to be
established. Performance audit of the programme covering the period 2006-10
revealed that Information Technology core infrastructure could not be
established and made operational even after lapse of four years. In absence of
availability of Government to Citizen (G2C) services State Wide Area



Chapter 1: Introduction

Network could not be utilized optimally, whereas the State Data Centre
project was still at the tendering stage. Data digitized for various departments
was neither used nor updated. Inter-connectivity amongst various departments
for information sharing and service delivery could also not be achieved in
absence of finalization of the State Mission Mode Projects.

(Paragraph 2.4)

1.6.5 Department centric audit of Animal Husbandry Department

The aim of the Animal Husbandry Department is to bring about qualitative
and quantitative improvement in production potential of livestock and disease
control. Audit of the department revealed that due to inadequate manpower,
financial resources and infrastructure, the objectives were not largely fulfilled
as target of vaccinations to encounter several livestock diseases and artificial
insemination were not achieved. Due to insufficient funds for medicines/
chemicals and a valid licence for departmental manufacturing of vaccines,
sufficient quantity of these was not available at the hospitals for treatment of
livestock. Scheme for creation of Self Help Groups to provide self
employment and prevent migration from villages was not implemented and
monitored properly. Backyard scheme for improving livelihood of Scheduled
Castes in poor category was also not successful due to improper planning.
(Paragraph 4.1)

1.7 Significant audit observations during Compliance Audit

Audit observed significant deficiencies in critical areas, which impact the
effectiveness of the State Government. Some important findings of the
compliance audit (23 paragraphs) have also been reported. The major
observations relate to:

. Non-compliance with rules and regulations.

. Audit against propriety and cases of expenditure without adequate
justification.

. Failure of oversight/governance.

. Persistent and pervasive irregularities.

1.7.1 Non-compliance with rules and regulations

For sound financial administration and control, it is essential that expenditure
conforms to financial rules, regulations and orders issued by the competent
authority. This helps in maintaining financial discipline and prevents
irregularities, misappropriation and frauds. This section of non-compliance
with rules and regulations brings out observations involving हैं 28.22 crore.
Some of these are as under.

1.7.2 Audit against propriety and cases of expenditure without adequate
justification

Authorisation of expenditure from public funds has to be guided by the
principles of propriety and efficiency of public expenditure. Authorities
empowered to incur expenditure are expected to enforce the same vigilance as
a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect of his own money.
Audit scrutiny revealed instances of impropriety and extra expenditure
amounting to % 47.56 crore. Some of the important audit findings are as
under.

Due to improper planning by the Forest Department, an irregular expenditure
of ® 40.10 crore was incurred on special plantation drive in Bundelkhand
region.

(Paragraph 3.2.1)

An avoidable extra payment of = 1.87 crore to a construction company was
made by the Irrigation Department for delayed payment of decretal amount.

(Paragraph 3.2.2)



Chapter 1: Introduction

Lack of planning by the Jail Administration and Reforms Department led to
wasteful expenditure of % four crore on an incomplete structure of a medium
security jail.

(Paragraph 3.2.3)

For want of operating staff, a Direct Circulatory Rotary rig machine and
auxiliary equipment, procured at a cost % 1.03 crore by the Chief Engineer,
Minor Irrigation Department in January 2009, was lying idle.

(Paragraph 3.2.4)

Non-identification of slum dwellers by the Urban Development Department
led to unfruitful expenditure of हैं 54.80 crore on construction of 274 dwelling
units under VAMBAY scheme.

(Paragraph 3.2.5)
1.7.3 Failure of oversight/ governance

The Government has an obligation to improve the quality of life of the people
in the area of health, education, development and upgradation of
infrastructure, public services, etc. Audit noticed instances where the funds
released by the Government for creating public assets, remained unutilized/
blocked or proved unfruitful/ unproductive due to indecisiveness, lack of
administrative oversight and concerted action at various levels. Cases of
failure of oversight/ governance noticed in audit amounted to % 58.43 crore.
Some important findings are as under.

Due to non-approval of rules by the Government, Revolving Fund could not
be operated by the Uttar Pradesh Council of Agriculture Research for
carrying out works such as strengthening of the agriculture related works and
creation of statistical data base, etc.

(Paragraph 3.3.1)

Due to non-approval of the revised estimates of a project on development of a
park in Mainpuri district by Horticulture Department and a decision to cancel
it half-way rendered the expenditure of 2.76 crore thereon wasteful.

(Paragraph 3.3.2)

Inadequate planning by the Chief Engineer, Saryu Pariyojna-I, Faizabad
under the Irrigation Department in construction of an Afflux Bund on left side
of the river Saryu resulted in unfruitful expenditure of ¥ 2.43 crore.

(Paragraph 3.3.3)

Failure to adhere to the technical instructions of the Indian Institute of
Technology, Roorkee by the Irrigation Department, rendered an expenditure



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

of हैं 224 crore on construction of Rohni weir in Maharajganj district
wasteful.

(Paragraph 3.3.4)

Inconsistency in the decision of the Jail Administration and Reforms
Department in site selection for a prison in Ambedkarnagar, rendered the
expenditure of €10 crore unfruitful apart from the delay in construction of the
prison.

(Paragraph 3.3.5)

Due to lack of coordination and monitoring, unfruitful expenditure of % 1.12
crore was incurred by the Women and Child Development Department on
construction of 125 buildings, besides construction of 227 buildings was not
taken up even after three years of their sanction.

(Paragraph 3.3.7)

Due to delay in handing over of stadia coupled with failure to appoint
trainers etc., by the Youth Welfare Department, an expenditure of % 18.31
crore on 50 stadia and purchase of sports material was rendered unfruitful.

(Paragraph 3.3.8)
1.7.4 Persistent and pervasive irregularities

An irregularity is considered persistent if it occurs year after year. It is
deemed pervasive when prevalent in the entire system. Recurrence of
irregularities, despite being pointed out in earlier audits is indicative of
slackness on the part of the executive and lack of effective monitoring. This
in turn encourages willful deviations from observance of rules/ regulations
and results in weakening of administrative structure. Audit observed instances
of persistent and pervasive irregularities of € 3.79 crore. Some important audit
findings are as under.

Irregular drawal of हैं 3.79 crore by Executive Engineers of Irrigation
Department from the treasuries and retaining it outside the Government
account put an avoidable interest burden of € 32 lakh on the Government.

(Paragraph 3.4.1)

1.8 Response of the departments to the draft Performance Reviews
and Audit Paragraphs

The draft performance reviews and audit paragraphs are forwarded to the
Principal Secretaries/ Secretaries of the departments concerned drawing their
attention to the audit findings and requesting them to send their responses
within six weeks. It is brought to their personal attention that in view of likely
inclusion of such paragraphs in the Audit Reports of the Comptroller and



Chapter 1: Introduction

Auditor General of India, which are placed before Uttar Pradesh Legislature,
it would be desirable to include their comments on the matter. They are also
advised to have meetings with the Principal Accountant General to discuss the
performance reviews/ draft audit paragraphs, proposed for Audit Reports. The
performance reviews/ draft audit paragraphs proposed for inclusion in this
report were forwarded to Principal Secretaries/ Secretaries concerned and
their replies and results of discussions have been incorporated in the Report at
appropriate places.

1.9 _ Follow up on Audit Reports

At the end of March 2010, 1,023 paragraphs/reviews were pending for
discussion in the Public Accounts Committee. These paragraphs/ reviews
pertained to the periods 1983-84 to 2007-08, except of the years 1997-98 and
2002-03. The discussion by the Public Accounts Committee on the
paragraphs/ reviews of the Audit Reports for the years 2004-05, 2005-06 and
2006-07 was in progress and those for 2007-08 and 2008-09 were yet to be
taken up



Chapter 2: Performance Audit

Chapter 2

Performance Audit

This chapter contains results of performance audit of Implementation of
Vidhan Mandal Kshetriya Vikas Nidhi, Urban Health Services, State Road
Fund Scheme and e-Governance in the Information and Technology
Department.

2.1___ Implementation of Vidhan Mandal Kshetriya Vikas Nidhi

Executive summary

With a view to enabling the Legislators to address the locally felt needs of the
people of their constituencies, the Government launched (1998-99) Vidhan
Mandal Kshetriya Vikas Nidhi (NIDHJ). The amount of annual grant for each
Legislator was हैं 1.25 crore (© one crore upto 2006-07). The performance
audit of the N/DHI covering the period 2005-10 during which 2,716.99
crore was spent, revealed the following.

¢ Due to delayed submission of the proposals by the Legislators, huge
amount remained unspent in the Personal Ledger Accounts at the close of
each financial year. At the close of March 2010, the overall unspent
balance amounted to = 385.58 crore. हैं 11.36 crore were also deposited
(2008-10) in Government’s revenue accounts.

e There were variations in the accounts maintained by the Commissioner,
Rural Development and District Rural Development Agencies as the
balances were carried forward either short or excess with net short
accounting of € 29.31 crore.

¢ Sanction to works did not relate to the locally felt needs exclusively. Out
of & 815.56 crore, हैं 669.02 crore (82 per cent) were spent on roads and
school buildings. In seven constituencies cent per cent funds were spent
on construction of school buildings.

¢ Due to delay in recommending the projects by the Legislators and their
sanction by District Rural Development Agencies and execution by
implementing agencies, a substantial numbers of projects spilled over.

¢ Against the set benchmarks in the guidelines, 334 inadmissible/ irregular
and non-durable projects costing हैं 11.78 crore were sanctioned and
executed.

¢ 575 projects costing = 10.98 crore were lying incomplete due to selection
of ineligible implementing agencies.

¢ 15,346 projects costing हैं 461.71 crore were completed but not handed
over to the local bodies for their upkeep and maintenance.



Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

2.41.1. Introduction

The Government, in 1998-99, launched the Vidhan Mandal Kshetriyva Vikas
Nidhi (NIDHI). Its objective was to enable 504 Legislators of the State to
address the locally felt needs of the people of their constituencies such as
construction of roads, school buildings, etc.’

As per the strategy adopted, the Government sanctioned grants at the rate of
हैं 1.25 crore (© one crore upto 2006-07) in two installments for each
Legislator and placed the amount at the disposal of the Chief Development
Officers of the districts to whom the Legislators recommended the works for
execution in their constituencies.

2.1.2. Organisational set-up

The Principal Secretary, Rural Development Department, assisted by the
Commissioner, Rural Development was responsible for implementation of the
NIDHI. At the district level, while the Chief Development Officers (CDOs)
were overall in-charge of the N/DHI, the Project Directors, District Rural
Development Agencies (DRDAs) were responsible for receipt and release of
funds and maintenance of their accounts. Panchayati Raj Institutions, Rural
Engineering Services, Public Works Department, etc., were the implementing
agencies.

2.1.3. Audit objectives

The main audit objectives were to examine whether:

¢ financial management resulted in economic, efficient and effective
utilisation of resources:

¢ works were planned, sanctioned and executed after requisite approvals
to meet the locally felt needs; and

¢ internal controls and monitoring was effective.

2.1.4. Scope of audit and methodology

Audit examined the records relating to the implementation of the N/DHI for
period 2005-10 in the office of the Commissioner, Rural Development at
Lucknow and 18 DRDAs’ out of 71, selected on the basis of statistical
sampling using Probability Proportionate to Size With Replacement method.

An entry conference was held in May 2010 with the Principal Secretary, Rural
Development Department. In the conference, the Audit Objectives, Criteria
and Scope of Audit were discussed.

Minor bridges, drinking water facilities, drains, public latrines, public library, cultural and sports activities,
purchase of furniture, books and computers for school and colleges, etc.

Agra, Allahabad, Barcilly, Bulandshahar, Chandauli, Etah, Gorakhpur, Jhansi, Lucknow, Mathura, Mau,
Meerut, Muzaffarnagar, Saharanpur, Sonebhadra, Srawasti, Unnao and Varanasi

Audit findings

2.1.5. Fund management

The Government sanctioned grants each year for each Legislator for transfer
to PLAs® in the Public Account. Transfers to PLAs were booked as capital
expenditure in the books of accounts maintained by the Principal Accountant
General (Accounts and Entitlement), Allahabad.

Out of % 2,896.75 crore credited to PLAs, = 2,716.99 crore were spent on the
NIDHI and & 385.58 crore were lying unspent in PLAs at the close of March
2010. Audit scrutiny revealed as under:

2.1.5.2 Slow progress of expenditure and huge unspent balances

The progress of expenditure was very slow due to delayed submission of
proposals by the Legislators for execution of works despite Government’s
instructions (October 2000) to the CDOs to approach the Legislators for early
submission of proposals. As a result, the projects could not be identified
leading to accumulation of huge unspent balance of % 385.58 crore at the
close of 2009-10.

Moreover, in 12 out of 18 test checked districts हैं 11.36 crore were deposited
in the revenue accounts of the Government during 2008-10 (Appendix-2.1.1)
under the orders (August 2008 and June 2009) of the Finance Department.

Personal Ledger Accounts: are banking accounts opened and maintained in the treasury.

13


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

The amount represented unspent balance of % 5.99 crore in respect of
incomplete works and हैं 5.37 crore for which no works were proposed by the
Legislators.

In view of the slow progress of expenditure during the entire review period,
increase of हैं 25 lakh in the annual allotment to each Legislator from
2007-08 proved unjustified.

2.1.5.3 Variations in the accounts

Variations in the opening and closing balances

PLA records of the Commissioner Rural Development, revealed that balances
were carried forward either short or excess. While at the end of 2005-06,
₹ 2.94 crore were carried forward in excess, during 2006-09 हैं 32.25 crore
were carried forward short leading to a net short accountal of ¥ 29.31 crore.
During test check of records of eight out of 18 districts, it was revealed that
amounts were carried forward short/ excess due to incorrect reporting of
balances by the DRDAs as mentioned below:

¢ Due to incorrect reporting by the DRDAs of five districts’, है 1.53
crore were accounted for in excess and हैं 3.22 crore short in the
accounts maintained by the Commissioner, Rural Development. In
three other districts’, deposit of हैं 3.69 crore in the Government
account was not accounted as the concerned DRDAs did not indicate
the amount due to absence of relevant column in the format prescribed
for reporting financial progress.

e In Agra district, हैं 63.49 lakh were transferred® to other districts
However, the Commissioner, Rural Development accounted for
₹ 69.49 lakh. Similarly, in Mathura district, DRDA received हैं 45.21
lakh from other districts which the Commissioner, Rural Development
did not account for in his books. Likewise, in district Sonebhadra,
DRDA received हैं 1.18 crore from other districts and released हैं 2.13
crore to them. The Commissioner, Rural Development did not account
for these amounts.

Incorrect reporting and variations in the accounts during the review period
indicated lax internal control and weak management

The Secretary, during discussions (October 2010), stated that the accounts
would be reconciled. The reply is not satisfactory as the Commissioner Rural
Development who as the nodal agency was responsible for compilation of
financial progress correctly and accurately, did not reconcile the accounts
during the entire review period.

‘ Agra: Short- ₹ै 40 lakh (2006-07) and excess ₹ 92 lakh (2007-08), Etah: short ₹ 2.42 crore (2009-10); Jhansi:
excess हैं 0.28 crore (2008-09); Meerut: short. हैं 40 lakh (2006-07) Sonebhadra: excess- हैं 33 lakh (2008-09).

Chapter 2: Performance Audit

2.1.5.4 Other irregularities
. Interest not deposited into the Government Account

As per the guidelines, interest received on the amounts deposited in banks by
the implementing agencies was to be returned to DRDAs for deposit in the
Government Account. However, two DRDAs’ did not deposit = 30.69 lakh
and retained it. The Government during discussions assured (October 2010)
that the amount of interest earned will be deposited into the treasury.

. Levy of service charge
The scheme guidelines prohibited payment of administrative/ centage charges
to the implementing agencies for services rendered by them.

However, five test-checked DRDAs paid service charge of 2.78 lakh*® to the
UP Electronics Corporation, Lucknow on purchase (cost हैं 42.56 lakh) of 38
Laptops, etc. The Secretary of the department, during discussions (October
2010), assured that service charge paid will be recovered.

2.1.6. Planning

Planning entails a clear definition of priorities by observing economy,
efficiency, effectiveness and accountability in delivery of improved quality of
services.

Audit observed that the scheme does not specify the mechanism to be adopted
to ensure participation of various constituents like active forums of residents,
local bodies, NGOs, etc., under Legislators’ constituencies in identifying and
recommending projects responsive to locally felt needs for balanced
development.

2.1.6.1 Delayed submission of proposals by the Legislators

Legislators in 18 test checked districts proposed 46,906 projects for execution
during 2005-10. In nine districts, where 20,688 projects were proposed, 9,545
proposals (46 per cent, cost: = 220.25 crore) were submitted in the second and
third quarter, 2,680 (13 per cent, cost: = 49.29 crore) in the last quarter and
346 (cost: हैं 7.66 crore) after lapse of the financial year (Appendix-2. 1.2). In
the remaining districts, the date of submission of 26,218 projects by
Legislators was not available. The Secretary, during discussions (October
2010), stated that orders were already issued to speed-up execution of the
projects.

Delayed submission of proposals by the Legislators delayed the execution of
the projects.

2.1.6.3 Delay in sanction of projects

The projects proposed by the Legislators were to be sanctioned by the
DRDAs within 45 days, if otherwise acceptable, after examination of the
estimates and physical survey of the proposed site.

Scrutiny revealed that delayed submission of detailed estimates by the
implementing agencies to the DRDAs, despite Government’s instructions
(October 2000) under which the CDOs were to convene monthly meetings
with them, eventually led to the delayed sanctioning of the projects. In nine
out of 18 test checked districts, 3,387 works (cost: हैं 84.84 crore) were
sanctioned with delays ranging between one to 90 days and 1,142 works (cost
& 32.29 crore) with delays above 90 days (Appendix-2. 1.5).

The Project Director, DRDA, Bareilly stated (June 2010) that one Assistant
Engineer and two Junior Engineers were unable to cope up with the increased
work load of examining estimates and carrying out survey of proposed site.

Thus, lack of coordination between the DRDAs and the implementing
agencies together with shortage of technical staff impacted upon timely
sanctioning of the projects.

2.1.6.4 Non-formation of committees of technical departments

The scheme guidelines provided for formation of a committee of technical
departments at district level. Its function was to examine the estimates of the
works and carry out site verification.

Audit observed that no such committee was constituted (2005-10) in any of
the test checked districts except at Saharanpur where the committee, though
formed, did not examine the estimates and carry out site verification during
2005-10.

The Secretary, during discussions (October 2010), stated that necessary
instructions to constitute the committees would be issued. The fact remained
that the implementation of the scheme was not effective in view of proper
identification and execution of the projects.

reference. In contravention to this, inadmissible, irregular and non-durable
projects were executed as discussed below:

scheme. The guidelines also prohibit construction of residential/ non-
residential buildings of the Central/ State Government and memorials, etc.

2.1.7.2 Projects lying incomplete due to wrong selection of implementing
agencies

As per the guidelines and subsequent Government instructions (July 2001),
the overall responsibility for the selection of the implementing agencies rested
with the CDOs who could select an agency after consultation with the
Technical Committee. As the committees were not formed, the CDOs were
devoid of technical advice leading to wrong selection of implementing
agencies, indicating failure of the evolved system.

Scrutiny of records in eight districts test-checked revealed that 1,158 projects
(cost: = 35.33 crore) were awarded (2007-10) to six agencies'” for execution
and हैं 28.77 crore was released to them. Out of 1,158 projects, 560 works
were completed (₹ 15.99 crore)'® and 575 were lying incomplete after

constructed during 2005-10 at a cost of % 9.90 crore. As a result, drawings and
cost of each category of projects varied to the extent of = 0.50 lakh and

during 2005-10. Year wise/ district wise details are given in Appendix-2. 1.8.

The Secretary, during discussions (October 2010), assured that projects would
be executed as per the standard models. Reply was not tenable as Government
instructions were not followed and remained ineffective altogether.

Chapter 2: Performance Audit

2.1.8. Internal control and monitoring

Scrutiny of the records of 18 districts test-checked revealed that there were
deficiencies in the monitoring and internal control as detailed below:

. Non-maintenance of registers of works

For improving the quality of monitoring of the projects, the Government
issued (October 2000) orders, inter alia, for maintaining a register allotting
separate pages for each Legislator, exhibiting name of projects, date of
proposal, date of sanction, estimated cost of projects, probable date of
completion, date of release of first instalment, date of start of works and dates
of inspections.

Scrutiny of the records of 18 districts test-checked, however, revealed that
except in Saharanpur, Sonebhadra and Srawasti, no other districts maintained
the prescribed register. In absence of this register, execution of projects could
not be monitored.

The Secretary, during discussions (October 2010), assured that the prescribed
register would be maintained.

. Inspection of works

CDO was required to inspect a minimum of 10 per cent projects during
execution. As records like scheduled programme of the inspection, follow up
actions, etc., were not maintained in any of 18 test-checked districts it could
not be ascertained in audit whether projects were inspected and what was the
result of such inspections and the follow up thereon.

The Secretary, during discussions (October 2010), assured that necessary
arrangements would be made for such inspections and their reports would be
maintained.

2.1.9. Maintenance of assets

The assets created under the N/DHI were to be handed over to the user
agencies” for maintenance/upkeep.

Scrutiny in 18 test-checked districts, however, revealed that only hand pumps
and solar lights were handed over to the local bodies while other assets” were
not handed over. Compilation of data of 18 districts and its analysis revealed
that out of 46,906 projects sanctioned during 2005-10, 15,346 works (cost:
% 461.71 crore) were completed but not handed over to the local bodies as
detailed in Appendix-2. 1.9,

Scrutiny further, revealed that in three districts’, 795 high mast lights were
installed at a cost of = 9.21 crore during 2005-10 (Appendix-2.1.10). These

2° Like Urban and Rural Local Bodies
2 Roads, bridges, barat ghar (excluding district Mathura), boundary walls, community centers, shamshan ghats,
toilets, yatri sheds, drains, rain basera, high mast/sodium light, pratikshalaya, retaining wall and submersible

etc,
Bareilly, Gorakhpur and Shrawasti

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

high mast lights were, however, not functional for want of electric
connections for the last two to four years as these were also not handed over
to local bodies, which was a pre requisite for electric connections and
maintenance. These lights were also not in good condition due to lack of
proper maintenance, as was noticed in one” district during joint inspection by
the audit and Junior Engineer of the DRDA.

Thus, people were deprived of the intended benefits of assets created due to
their non-handing over to the local bodies. Besides, expenditure of हैं 9.21
crore on installation of high mast lights was rendered unfruitful.

The Secretary, during discussions (October 2010), stated that action will be
taken for early handing over of the assets. He also stated that instructions
would be issued to the CDOs for early handing over of these high mast lights
to the local bodies to ensure electric connection and maintenance.

The fact remained that funds were infused into the existing system without
complying with the systemic arrangements to monitor their effective
utilization and maintenance thereafter. As a result, these assets were left
uncared for and their maintenance was not ensured to strengthen effective
utilisation of the NZDHI funds by the concerned CDOs.

2.1.10. Conclusion

There was considerable delay in recommending projects by the Legislators.
The delay resulted in slow pace in utilisation of fund leading to accumulation
of huge amounts in the Personal Ledger Accounts at the end of the financial
year. A mechanism for assessing/ identifying local need based projects was
also absent as a result of which the objective of balanced development could
not be achieved. A substantial portion of the N/DHI/ was spent on construction
of roads and school buildings while the expenditure on other issues such as
health, sanitation drinking water, etc., was negligible. Besides, there were
cases of inadmissible, irregular and non-durable projects. The funds were
teleased to the educational institutions without any limit and in some cases,
substantial funds were given. Besides, the assets were not handed over for
their maintenance.

2.1.11. Recommendations

e The Government should ensure that projects are sanctioned timely and
executed. Efforts should be made to complete the spill over projects after
review.

¢ The Government should ensure participation of various constituents of
residents, local bodies, non-government organizations, etc., for proper
identification of projects for execution under the N/DHI.

¢ Prescribing financial limits similar to MPLADS scheme for a project or a
school building should be considered.

¢ Government should ensure handing over of the assets created to the local
bodies for their proper maintenance.

मे. Bareilly

Chapter 2: Performance Audit

2.2. Urban Health Services

Executive Summary

With a view to providing specialized medical services to the people, the
Government decided to upgrade health facilities in urban areas during the
Eleventh Plan (2007-12) period. A sum of % 4,312 crore was spent during
2007-10. The performance audit of up-gradation of Urban Health Services
revealed the following

e Provisions for preparation of budget were not followed and the plan of
action for procurement of equipment, major construction works and
fixtures, etc., was not finalized. This led to savings of % 1,243.63 crore
during 2007-10.

e During 2007-08, advanced medical devices like CT scans, C-arm Image
Intensifier, reagents, X-ray films, etc., were not purchased. As a result,
% 147.27 crore, lying unspent in the Personal Ledger Account, were frozen
by the Government and deposited as revenue in the Consolidated Fund.

¢ There was an overall shortage of 11 per cent to 77 per cent in various
cadres of medical staff. The shortages impacted adversely the
department’s ability to cater to the medical care needs of the people.

¢ None of the seven 100 bedded hospitals and two 300 bedded hospitals
were completed by the target date due to lack of monitoring by the
Director General, Medical and Health Services, Lucknow despite release
of almost entire estimated cost by the Government. Likewise, while
construction works of three mortuaries was incomplete, the work on
remaining three could not be taken up due to land disputes.

¢ Though the land was not available for construction of children’s hospital
in Lucknow and for TB-cum-General Hospital at Gorakhpur districts, हैं 17
crore were transferred to the construction agencies.

¢ Due to improper planning and ineffective monitoring, the civil works of
three trauma centres were incomplete and two completed centres could
not be made functional due to non-posting of medical staff. Besides, the
controlling officers purchased equipment (cost: हैं 2.57 crore) which were
not immediately required.

¢ The department incurred extra expenditure of f 3.60 crore on purchase of
Anti Rabies Vaccine

2.2.1 Introduction

The Government runs a network of district hospitals and super speciality
hospitals for providing health services to the people living in urban areas.

Recognizing the need to remove the inadequacies in the existing network, the
Government decided to upgrade health facilities in urban areas during
Eleventh Plan Period (2007-12) for providing specialised medical care.

At the Government level, the Principal Secretary, Medical Health and Family
Welfare and at the Department level, the Director General, Medical and
Health Services, Lucknow (DGMHS) assisted by Additional Directors at the
Divisional level and Chief Medical Officers (CMOs), Chief Medical
Superintendents (CMSs) at the District level,

implementing the Government’s decision.

are responsible for

Records relating to upgradation of health services in urban areas, as envisaged
in Eleventh Plan, were test checked from March 2010 to September 2010 in
the office of DGMHS, Lucknow. The entry conference was held in June 2010
and the exit conference in December 2010 with the Secretary of the
Department. In the exit conference, audit findings were discussed and their
response incorporated, wherever necessary.

2.2.2 Financial performance

The Government, based on the proposals of DGMHS made provision in the
budget and allotted funds for running the health services in the State.

Records revealed that provision for pay and allowances was made on the basis
of sanctioned strength, instead of on the basis of actual men-in-position.
As there was a huge shortfall in medical and para medical staff, vis-a-vis, the
sanctioned strength, this resulted in savings of हैं 947.44 crore in revenue
section during 2007-10,

Besides, due to non-finalization of plan of action by DGMHS for procurement
of equipment and fixtures (% 63.76 crore) and for execution of major
construction works (® 38.37 crore), there were savings of हैं 296.19 crore in
capital section during the same period.

The Secretary stated (December 2010) that the budget provision was made in
anticipation of appointments of the medical staff. Reply is not tenable as
budget proposals were not prepared in consonance with the provisions of the
Budget Manual.

In respect of savings under capital section, the Secretary admitted that
financial sanctions were not accorded due to non-finalisation of the plan of
action.

2.2.2.2 Non-procurement of equipment

Procurement process entails finalization of specifications of the equipment,
inviting tenders and obtaining approval of the competent authority.

The Government sanctioned (2007-08) % 147.27 crore for procurement of
advanced medical devices like CT scans, C-arm Image Intensifier, regents,
X-ray films, etc. Pending completion of the procurement process, DGMHS
deposited the amount in the PLA of the Chattrapati Shahuji Maharaj Medical
University to facilitate its withdrawals in subsequent financial years.

However, before the procurement process could be completed and the amount
drawn, the Government froze (August 2008) the unspent balance of € 147.27
crore of the PLA and DGMHS accordingly, transferred (2008-09) the amount
to the Consolidated Fund of the State.

Thus, due to non-finalisation of the purchase process and the transfer of
unspent balance of f 147.27 crore of PLA to the Consolidated Fund not only
inflated the expenditure during 2007-08 but also inflated the receipts of the
Department by an equal amount during 2008-09. Besides, various activities of
procurement of advanced medical devices under the Eleventh Five Year Plan
suffered a set back.

The Secretary, in reply, stated (December 2010) that the specifications of the
equipment were finalized and the tenders invited pending approval by the
Central Purchase Committee and High Power Purchase Committee. He also
stated that the Government, meantime, froze the unspent balances of the PLA.
The reply was not tenable as DGMHS did not make demand for funds
subsequently, as provided in the Government Order freezing the amount.

2.2.3. Shortage of medical and para-medical staff

There had been an overall shortfall, ranging between 11 per cent and

To overcome the shortfall, the Eleventh Plan provided for establishment of a
selection board for recruitment of medical officers. The board was, however,
not established as of November 2010 as the proposal (February 2009) was not
approved by the Uttar Pradesh Public Service Commission. However, no
reasons were cited for non-approval.

As a result, the existing network of the district hospitals at divisional level
was running with shortages of Uro Surgeons, Plastic Surgeons, Nephrologists
and Neuro Surgeons and at the district level with the shortages of
Cardiologists, Medical Officers for Skin/ENT, Radiologists, Pathologists,
Pediatricians, etc.

Shortage of human resources impacted adversely department’s ability to cater
to the medical care needs of the urban people.

2.2.4. Programme implementation

The Government identified the priorities for upgradation of health services
during the Eleventh Plan period and decided to create urban health care
infrastructure which, inter alia, included establishing 100 bedded combined
hospitals, 100 bedded children hospitals, 300 bedded combined hospitals, etc.

2.2.4.1 Delay in completion of hospitals and mortuaries

With a view to providing health care facilities, the Government decided to
construct seven 100 bedded combined hospitals in each of the seven newly
created districts”, at an estimated cost of f 91.92 crore and released (2006-11)
% 88.55 crore’ to the construction agencies for their completion between
December 2007 and October 2009, subsequently extended to December 2010.
However, none of these hospitals were completed by the target date. The

The Eleventh Plan also provided for establishment of five 300 bedded

work in remaining Auraiya, Kushinagar and Sant Kabir Nagar districts had
not been commenced due to land disputes (September 2010).

Thus, due to lack of administrative oversight, completion of works was
delayed. Besides, the objective of providing health care facilities, etc., to the
people of urban areas, as envisaged in the Eleventh Plan, was not realised.

DGMHS attributed the reasons for the delay in completion of the works to the
shortage of engineering staff* which eventually led to non-monitoring of the
works.

The Secretary, in reply, stated (December 2010) that action was being taken
to complete construction works expeditiously. The reply is not acceptable as
the works were incomplete due to failure to monitor slow progress of works
despite release of 96 per cent to 100 per cent of the estimated cost to the
construction agencies and release of money without specifying even the target
date of completion in one case.

2.2.5 Purchase of Anti Rabies Vaccine

The Government’s Purchase Policy (April 2004) prohibited DGMHS to
purchase medicines under quantity contract for which rate contracts existed.

Records revealed that DGMHS, Lucknow did not follow the purchase policy.
The Employees State Insurance (ESI) had a rate contract of € 207.41 paise per
vial with Messers Aventis Pharma and Ranbaxy for supply of Anti Rabies
Vaccine" valid for the period from September 2005 to August 2007. Despite
this, DGMHS invited (January 2007) tenders from these firms and placed
orders for supply of nine lakh vials at the rate of € 247.38 paise per vial. Non-
observance of the purchase policy resulted in avoidable extra payment of

2.2.6 Conclusion

The upgradation projects taken up under Eleventh Plan initiative were either
incomplete or not taken up. Money was transferred to the construction
agencies notwithstanding the fact that even land for construction was not
available and even without prescribing the time-frame for completion of the
construction works. The medical equipment was procured even when the civil
works were incomplete. The internal control of the department was also weak.

2.2.7 Recommendations

¢ The Government should focus on completion of the works which were
planned in the Eleventh Five Year Plan so that the medical health care
facilities in the urban areas are upgraded.

¢ Vacant posts of the medical and para-medical staff should be filled up
early.

e The Government should strengthen the internal controls and
monitoring in the department.

Chapter 2: Performance Audit

2.3 Implementation of State Road Fund scheme
Executive Summary

With a view to keeping all the State roads free of potholes and patches by
continuously maintaining and repairing, the Government created (1998) State
Road Fund and levied additional sales tax on diesel and petrol forming the
receipts of the Fund. The Public Works Department, which is the nodal
department, maintained roads falling under its jurisdiction only under the
State Road Fund scheme. Performance audit of the State Road Fund scheme,
covering the period 2005-10 was conducted to examine the economy,
efficiency and effectiveness of activities keeping in view the objectives of the
scheme. The major audit findings are as under.

¢ Objectives of the scheme were not realised fully, mainly due to inadequate
planning, poor financial management and lax internal control mechanism.

¢ Identification of roads for renewal and maintenance was done without
adequate data and lacked transparency. Fifty-two per cent to 59 per cent
of roads, due for renewal were not considered for maintenance.

¢ PWD did not consider maintenance of roads belonging to the other
departments/ bodies though the scheme envisaged coverage of all State
roads.

¢ Additional realisation of tax of = 1,003 crore and ¥ 998.41 crore, lying
unspent since April 2005 was not utilised on maintenance of roads.

¢ There were also cases of double sanctions, wrong selection of works and
parking of funds in PLA to avoid lapse of budget.

2.3.1. Introduction

The State of Uttar Pradesh has 3.37 lakh km of roads of various categories as
of March 2010, out of which 1.63 lakh km! were under the jurisdiction of
Public Works Department and the remaining under other departments

With a view to fulfilling the objectives enunciated in the State Road
Development Policy, 1998, of according adequate priority to proper repair
and maintenance of roads at requisite intervals, the State Road Fund (SRF)
scheme was formulated (January 2000). For this purpose, Sales tax on petrol
was raised from 14 to 20 per cent and on diesel from 16 to 20 per cent ad
valorem and the additional amount so realised was to be assigned to the SRF
to the extent the Government deemed fit. The Rules of the Fund were framed
by the Government in January 2000 and a State Road Fund Management

1 State Highways: 7922 km; Major District Roads: 7071 km, Other District Roads: 31238 km and Village Roads:
117199 km

33


Audit Report (Cis

il) for the year ended 31 March 2010

Committee (SRFMC)” was constituted (May 2002) for granting approval to
the criteria and items to be undertaken under the SRF.

2.3.2. Organizational set-up

The Principal Secretary, PWD at the Government level and the Engineer-in-
Chief (E-in-C), Development at the departmental level assisted by 22 Chief
Engineers (CEs) including 12 Zonal CEs and 31 Superintending Engineers
(SEs) implemented the scheme. The works were executed through 165
divisions headed by the Executive Engineers (EEs) in 71 districts.

2.3.3. Audit objectives

The objectives of performance audit were to assess whether:

. SRF scheme was managed in accordance with the State Road
Fund Rules, 2000;

¢ Identification and selection of works were carried out on the basis
of reliable and accurate road data and in accordance with the
rules;

. Execution of works was carried out effectively, economically and
efficiently; and

. Monitoring system was adequate and effective to achieve the
desired objectives.

2.3.4. Scope and methodology of audit

Records relating to implementation of SRF for the period 2005-10 were test
checked between March 2010 and July 2010 at the PWD Secretariat, in the
offices of E-in-C, three CEs* out of 12 and three SEs* out of 31, in 14
districts’ out of 71 districts. Besides, information relating to SRF was
collected from the Finance Department. The districts were selected through
Probability Proportional to Size without Replacement method of statistical
sampling.

Audit objectives were discussed (May 2010) with the Principal Secretary,
PWD during the entry conference. In the exit conference (October 2010) held
with Principal Secretary PWD, the audit findings were discussed. Replies
received (October 2010) from Government were incorporated at appropriate
places in the review

Tourism Department and Finance Department, two representatives of Truck Motors Association, two Members

Audit Findings
2.3.5 Financial Management
2.3.5.1 Non-operation of the SRF

SRF Rules, 2000 provide that the additional tax levied and realised on first
sale of petrol and diesel would be credited to SRF account maintained under
the Public Account to the extent the Government deemed fit. For utilising the
funds, the State Government would make the provision in the annual budget
of the PWD under the Grant® No. 58. At the end of the financial year, the
amount so spent out of the budget provision was to be debited to the SRF and
credited to the Grant No. 58. The Government did not operate SRF account
from April 2005 and the unutilised amount of = 998.41 crore was lying in the
account since then. In March 2009, the Government decided to abolish the
Fund. Non-operation of the fund rendered the operation of the SRF scheme as
envisaged, opaque as discussed below:

Additional tax on first sale of petrol and diesel was levied for a definite
purpose and the proceeds were to be applied for such purposes only. The
proceeds were to be parked to the extent determined by the Government in a
specifically created SRF and this fund utilised exclusively for repair,
maintenance and strengthening of roads. No amount was credited to the fund
out of the additional income during the period 2005-10. The extent of
additional tax realised, amount utilised and balance was not ascertainable.
However, the amount of additional tax realised, as worked out in audit
amounted to = 4603. 18 crore as indicated in Table 1.

Implementation of the SRF scheme
2.3.6 Planning and Monitoring
Approval of works under SRF scheme

As per SRF Rules 2000, the fund was to be utilised for maintenance and
repair/strengthening of all State roads. SRFMC was empowered to decide the
items and criteria for utilisation of the funds. No criteria for selection of roads
for maintenance, repair/strengthening were laid down by SRFMC. Audit
scrutiny of the methodology adopted for identification and selection of roads
under SRF scheme revealed the following:-

2.3.6.1 Lack of transparency in identification and selection of roads

The State Government framed Uttar Pradesh Road Development Policy, 1998
for better utilization of State resources for maintenance and repair of road
network. As per paragraph 12.1 of the Policy, a computerized data bank,
having the details of every road, its width, thickness of its crust, composition
of crust, engineering properties of earth in sub-grade, details of culverts,
bridges and permanent land, road history etc, was to be established to
implement the construction and maintenance work in a planned manner. In
addition to this, traffic density in terms of commercial vehicles per day and
passenger car units and details of accidents on the road would also be kept in
the computerised data bank. Besides, a computerised Management
Information System (MIS) would be developed at the Divisional, Circle,
Zonal Chief Engineer, Engineer-in-chief and Government levels.

Audit observed that the PWD had neither maintained a computerised data
bank of all its roads nor developed any MIS for identification and transparent

38



Cancellation of
sanctioned works
due to defective
proposals, double
sanctions, etc.,
resulted in
surrender of
funds

Chapter 2: Performance Audit

selection of roads for maintenance, repair and strengthening. PWD is the
nodal authority for proposing maintenance and repair /strengthening of roads
and these proposals are examined at the departmental level. The department
allocates the amount category-wise depending upon the nature of the work.
These category-wise proposals are sent to SRFMC for approval. In view of
this, the system of identification and selection of roads remained questionable.

In reply, Government stated (October 2010) that as computerised data bank
was under preparation, roads were selected under SRF scheme on the basis of
information given by the divisions. The fact remained that roads were
identified/ selected without cross checking of the proposals of the divisions in
the absence of requisite of data bank.

2.3.6.2 Defective sanctions

E-in-Cs’ circular (July 2005) for maintenance, repairs/ strengthening and
widening of roads provides that the proposals for maintenance of roads should
be prepared on the basis of actual survey of the road with full justification and
its requirement. It was observed from the records of E-in-C that 172 works
sanctioned during the period 2005-10 were cancelled subsequently, due to
various reasons including defective proposals, double sanctions, absence of
co-ordination amongst executing agencies and works sanctioned under other
schemes. Some of the reasons of defective proposals were problem of water

logging, length of the road actually short, portions of roads actually not
existing/ constructed, excessively damaged, work not required, less crust, etc
Details of cancelled works are given in Table 5.

2.3.6.6 State roads not maintained

The State had 3.37 lakh km roads of different categories as of March 2010,
out of which 1.63 lakh km roads were under the jurisdiction of PWD and the
remaining 1.74 lakh km roads under other departments/bodies such as Mandi
Parishad, Rural Engineering Services, Zila Parishad and Cane Development
Department, etc.

Although the SRF scheme was created for maintenance of all State roads, the
Government failed to maintain a length of 1.74 lakh km roads, pertaining to
departments/bodies other than the PWD as only proposals relating to PWD
roads alone were submitted and considered by SRFMC during (2005-10).
PWD had not coordinated with these departments, etc. to obtain their
proposals for maintenance and repair of the roads.

2.3.6.7 Monitoring

As per paragraph 6(2) of SRF Rules 2000, E-in-C, PWD is responsible for
proper execution of maintenance and repair of the roads under SRF,
monitoring of the scheme and submission of progress report to the
Government at the end of each financial year. E-in-C is also required to
maintain details of expenditure incurred under SRF scheme separately and
submit Monthly Progress Report (MPR) of physical and financial progress to
the Government

For effective monitoring and control over financial and physical progress,
E-in-C directed (June 2007) all EEs to maintain records/registers/ledgers
prescribed in Financial Hand Book Vol.-VI. Records of 14 divisions test-
checked, revealed that the Works Abstract'®, Register of Works'’,
Contractor's Ledger'*, Road Inspection Register and Road Metal Return
prescribed for control over advances and material given to contractor for
work, expenditure incurred on a particular work, consumption of material
received by junior engineers and complete history of roads, were not
maintained. This resulted in ineffective monitoring, evaluation and reporting
of the financial and physical progress of the works by the Executive
Engineers. In reply, Government stated (October 2010) that Zonal Chief
Engineers have been directed for proper keeping of records at divisional level.

2.3.7.2 Avoidable expenditure
Avoidable extra expenditure on three roads

The basic principle for strengthening of the road stipulates that the bituminous
macadam (BM) should be laid over the non-bituminous base only when the
crust thickness of the road is sufficient to sustain the traffic load. BM laid
over the deficient crust is not sustainable in the long run. As per Indian Road
Congress (IRC) Guidelines, BM should be laid only when base of non-
bituminous crust thickness was not less than 37.5 cm even if the traffic
density of the roads was minimum and soil strength of the sub-grade was rich.
Instead of BM and SDBC, provision of First Coat Painting (P1) followed by
Second Coat Painting (P2) or Premix Carpet (PC) with Seal Coat should be
made as wearing course.

Scrutiny of the records of works of the test-checked districts, sanctioned
under SRF scheme revealed that in the three road works in the districts Hardoi
and Muzaffarnagar, a provision for BM and SDBC was made instead of PC
with Seal Coat though the base of these roads had non-bituminous crust with

20742 km renewed under SRF and remaining through other schemes

44


Chapter 2: Performance Audit

thickness ranging between 23 and 34 cm. Expenditure of % 4.35 crore was
incurred on these roads in violation of IRC guidelines.

The excess expenditure of = 4.35 crore as detailed in Table 11 could have
been saved by following the IRC guidelines.

2.3.7.3 Expenditure incurred before technical sanction to works

Paragraph 375 of Financial Handbook Volume-VI provides that no work
should be commenced until detailed estimates are approved by the competent
authority. As per E-in-C’s Circular (January 2003), time period to accord
technical sanction to the detailed estimates of a sanctioned work was 15, 30
and 45 days for EEs, SEs and CEs respectively. The sanction order issued for
each work also stipulates that no expenditure should be incurred before grant
of technical sanction to the detailed estimates of the work.

Scrutiny of the records of the works sanctioned under SRF scheme, in five
divisions” revealed that technical sanction to 21 works was not accorded even
after lapse of 10 to 23 months after the month of their financial sanction and
an expenditure of = 3.52 crore was incurred on these works as of March 2010.
In case of another 34 works, technical sanctions were accorded with delays
ranging from 6 to 23 months and expenditure of 21.47 crore was incurred
prior to issue of technical sanctions as detailed in Appendix-2.3.3. Incurring
of expenditure prior to accord of technical sanction apart from violation of the
financial rules also vitiates the sanctity of technical sanction. In reply, the
Government intimated (October 2010) that directions have been issued to the
concerned CEs to investigate the matter.

2.3.9. Ineffective Quality Control

Research and Quality Promotion Cell (RQPC) of the department had been
established for conducting test of the samples of the material required for
construction to maintain the quality of work. As per the Government orders
(August 1996), it is mandatory for PWD divisions to send 25 per cent of the
samples of material to be used in the works to the RQPC to ascertain its
quality. The material was to be used only after receipt of the results of such
tests.

In six” out 14 divisions test-checked, no records were, however, maintained
by the divisions regarding samples of material sent to RQPC for quality
control test. As per information furnished by RQPC, (July 2010) regarding

tests carried out on the material during the period 2005-10, only seven
divisions had sent 133 samples relating to 17 works. Details are given in
Table 15.

The above is indicative of ineffective quality control in the department. In

all CEs to follow the Government order strictly.

2.3.10. Conclusion

Selection of roads for renewal was not transparent due to non-availability of
data of all roads. There were cases of selection of ineligible works, pre-mature
renewal of roads, adoption of rich specification in violation of IRC’s
recommendations, commencement of works without TS, deficient contract
management. Also, internal controls were lax as quality control norms were
not adhered to, to prevent utilisation of sub-standard material. Thus, the
objective of keeping all the State roads free of potholes and patches by
continuously maintaining and repairing was not achieved to the desired
extent.

2.3.11. Recommendations

e A comprehensive and computerised Data Base for selection of eligible
roads should be maintained and updated regularly. A unique code to each
road should be assigned to avoid wrong selection of works, issue of
double sanctions, repetition of works and execution under other schemes;

¢ Co-ordination committees at District/ Zonal level may be constituted for
preparation of priority list of roads of all categories to be undertaken
annually for maintenance, etc. :

¢ Internal controls for quality control and contract management should be
strengthened; and

¢ Investigations instituted as a result of audit findings should be completed
early and remedial action taken.

Audit Report

for the year ended 31 March 2010

Department of Information Technology and Electronics

2.4 IT Audit on ‘Preparedness for e-Governance in the State of Uttar
Pradesh’

e-Governance involves focusing on the use of Information Technology to
assist in the transformation of government structures and operations to
provide Efficient, Convenient & Transparent Services to the Citizens &
Businesses

Executive Summary

With a view to ‘Make all Government services accessible to the common man
in his locality, through common service delivery outlets and ensure efficiency,
transparency & reliability of such services at affordable costs to realise the
basic needs of the common man’ National e-Governance Programme (NeGP)
was launched in May 2006.

In order to achieve the above objective, under the NeGP, some pre-requisites
in the area of IT core infrastructure viz. establishment of State Wide Area
Network (SWAN), State Data Center (SDC) and Common Service Centers
(CSCs) was essential and needed to be in place to provide citizen centric
services to the targeted common man in the field of 17 Mission Mode Projects
(Departments) identified by the State Government.

A Performance Audit of the “Preparedness for e-Governance in the State of
Uttar Pradesh (UP) revealed that the implementation of the NeGP in selected
17 MMPs was far behind from the satisfactory level of completion and
implementation in the State. The Performance Audit brings out the following
major deficiencies:

. Implementation of the projects was far from satisfactory as neither had
the IT core infrastructure been established, nor was it made
operational as planned.

. In absence of the Agreement with the NIC and BSNL 50 SWAN POP
sites could not be made operational. Optical Fiber Connectivity which
was to be ensured up to the Block level was available only at the
District headquarters

. Against the target of 17,909 CSCs to be established (upto April 2009)
in the State only 4909 CSCs could be established (July 2010) as CSCs
failed to operate in absence of availability of Government to Citizen
(G2C) services.

. State Data Centre could not be made operational even after lapse of
two years as selection process for appointment of Service Provider
was not finalized.

50

2.4.1 Introduction

To increase transparency, efficiency and effectiveness in delivery of citizen
services, the National e-Governance Plan (NeGP), comprising 27 Mission
Mode Projects' (MMPs) and 8 components, was approved by the Government
of India (GOI) on May 18, 2006. The NeGP vision was to ‘Make all
Government services accessible to the common man in his locality, through
common service delivery outlets and ensure efficiency, transparency &
reliability of such services at affordable costs to realise the basic needs of the
common man’. Under NeGP, it was proposed to create State Data Centre to
consolidate services, applications and infrastructure to provide efficient
electronic delivery of Government to Government (G2G), Government to
Citizen (G2C) and Government to Business (G2B) services. These services
were to be rendered through common delivery platform seamlessly supported
by core Connectivity Infrastructure viz. State Wide Area Network (SWAN)
and Common Service Centre (CSC) connectivity extended up to village level

The creation of core and support infrastructure were to enable web based
services for anytime anywhere access and thereby changing the delivery of

1 MMP: Implies projects having well defined objectives, scope, service levels, milestones and timelines for
implementation.

51


Audit Report (Cis

il) for the year ended 31 March 2010

identified services by Government. Out of the 27 MMPs under the NeGP, the
State identified 177 MMPs.

Under the e-District MMP the State identified 10 services to be delivered
electronically viz. Issue of Certificates, Pensions (Social Welfare Pension),
Revenue Court, Government Dues and Recoveries, Public Distribution
System (Ration card related), RTI Services, Employment, Election, Licenses,
Utility Services comprising of 32 sub-services (Appendix-2.4.1) of which
only 22 could be enabled.

2.4.2 State e-Governance vision

Vision: Using information technology for empowering the masses through
easier access to information, transforming government process for better
service delivery and as a vehicle for economic development of the state."

To achieve the State vision and objectives of the NeGP, the e-Governance
roadmap of the State (November 2006) and 55 Departments of the State (18
intensive study and 37 extensive study) was prepared (February 2007) by
consultant M/s Price Water House Cooper The objective of the departmental
study was to undertake "As-Is" assessment of Information Technology usage
and level of computerization in the Departments and prepare five year e-
Governance roadmap. The departmental e-Governance roadmap was designed
with a view to ensuring

¢ Good governance by providing need based citizen centric services;

¢ Cost economy through development and use of common infrastructure
and adherence to common policies;

¢ Business Process Re-engineering (BPR) and Administrative reforms;
¢ Change management through capacity building;
e Fast implementation of project (Mission mode);

¢ Standardization of approach and design across all departmental
projects.

2.4.3 Citizen expectations from e-governance

As per the e-Governance Roadmap of the State, citizen expectations from the
e-governance are summarized as under:

¢ Easy and faster information access about the government and its
services:

State MMPs (Departments) : Revenue, Tax and Registration, Transport, Agriculture, Home, Finance, Urban
Development, Panchayati Raj, Labour, Medical and Health, Food and civil supply, Social welfare, Woman
welfare and Child Development, Backward Class Welfare, Minorities Welfare, Secondary Education and
Handicap Department

52


Chapter 2: Performance Audit
¢ Flexibility in accessing the services of the government, with services
available where and when there is a demand;
¢ Responsiveness to feedback about the content and quality of services:

¢ The citizen should be able to get the services from the government in
an integrated manner.

The IT audit was conducted from April 2010 to July 2010 to evaluate the
performance of the NeGP scheme as implemented by the State. The period of
audit coverage was from inception of the scheme in 2006-07 to March 2010.
Records were examined at

The audit objectives were to assess:

. whether the planning process was effective for implementation
and operation of the schemes;

e economy, efficiency and effectiveness of the Information System
acquired and installed for rendering services as envisaged in
project guidelines;

. whether IT delivery and support mechanism was effective and
functional; and

. whether monitoring and evaluation mechanism was effective.

2.4.7 Audit findings

2.4.7.1 Lack of planning resulting in ineffective implementation and
operation of projects

With a view to making Government services accessible to the common man
in the concerned locality, the NeGP envisions three pillars of e-Governance
infrastructure:

i) State Wide Area Network (SWAN) for establishing connectivity,

ii) State Data Centre (SDC) for centralized secure data storage/retrieval,
and

iii) Common Service Centers (CSCs) for delivery of web enabled any time
anywhere access to information and services. Departmental services
were to be provided by the Departments selected under the State MMPs
and services not covered under the MMPs were to be made available

Gautam Budhnagar, Ghaziabad, Gorakhpur, Raebareli, Sitapur and Sultanpur

Agriculture Department, Food & Civil Supply Department, Panchayati Raj Department and Woman Welfare &
Child Development, Department

Caste Certificate, Domicile Certificate, Widow pension, Old Age pension, Ration card and Grievance filing.

34


Chapter 2: Performance Audit

under e-District MMP operational in six’ pilot districts of the State.
Synchronization in implementation of core infrastructure and
availability of citizen centric Government services was necessary to
achieve effective service delivery mechanism.

However, audit revealed that the progress of project implementation was far
from satisfactory as neither had the IT core infrastructure been established,
nor was it made operational as planned or ensured availability of services to
the citizens in the State due to ineffective operation of projects as detailed in
succeeding paragraphs.

2.4.7.2 Non-execution of service level agreements for SWAN

The objective of State Wide Area Network (SWAN) was to create a secure
close user group government network to interconnect State Headquarters with
the District Headquarters, Tehsil and Block headquarters with 2 Mpbs
bandwidth connectivity for the purpose of delivering G2G and G2C services.
As per the SWAN guidelines (October 2004) for establishment, operation and
maintenance of SWAN an end-to-end Service Level Agreement (Agreement)
was required to be executed with the implementing agency as well as with the
bandwidth service provider for provisioning of minimum 2Mpbs leased line
connectivity upto the Block level.

Department selected NIC as the primary implementing agency (October 2004)
and BSNL (November 2006) as the Bandwidth service provider. NIC was
responsible for establishment and operation of 885 SWAN Point of Presence
(POPs) at the State Capital (01), respective Districts (70), Tehsils (240) and
Blocks (574).

However, against the provisions of the guidelines no Agreement was signed
either with the BSNL or with the NIC (as of July 2010). In absence of
Agreement, key service level indicators to be observed by the implementing
agency and bandwidth operator could not be fixed as such observance of the
same could not be ensured. As a result;

¢ Out of 885 POPs established by NIC, 50 POPs could not be made
operational (commissioned) as of July 2010. In 40 POPs BSNL failed to
provide 2Mpbs lease line connectivity due to distance being more than 5
km between the POP site and BSNL exchange. Remaining 10 POPs could
not be commissioned due to technical problems (modem/line problem).
BSNL demanded €80.00 lakh for installing additional equipment from the
Department to resolve the issue. Department referred the issue to the DIT,
GOI, and on the directives (May 2009) of the Project Management Unit,
GOI refused to release the funds on the grounds that 2Mpbs connectivity
upto the Block POPs was to be ensured by the BSNL for which only
rental charges were to be paid. Neither the BSNL installed the equipment

Gautam Budhnagar, Ghaziabad, Gorakhpur, Raebareli, Sitapur, and Sultanpur

on
On


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2010

to the make the POPs operational nor could any penalty be imposed by the
Department.

¢ Without ensuring connectivity from the BSNL exchange NIC installed the
network hardware at the 40 POPs which remained unutilised since
installation (2008-2009). This is reflective of lack of coordination between
NIC and BSNL.

¢ 2Mpbs Optical Fiber Connectivity (OFC) upto the Block headquarters was
to be ensured by the BSNL. However, in the test checked Gorakhpur
district, out of 21 POPs commissioned OFC connectivity was provided
only at District headquarter, Managed Lease Line Network (MLLN)
connectivity was provided in one Tehsil and two Blocks and in the rest 17
POPs non-MLLN connectivity was provided. Absence of OFC not only
reduces the speed of the network but also involves installation of one
modem for MLLN connectivity and two modems for non-MLLN
connectivity.

The Department could not initiate any action or impose penalty against BSNL
in absence of SLA which is reflective of lack of planning at the highest level.

In reply, the Government stated (November 2010) that agreement with the
BSNL was signed (November 2010) and that with the NIC was under process.

2.4.7.3 Non-operation of Common Service Centres (CSCs)

The front-end interface of the NeGP with the rural citizens were the CSCs
through which the Government services along with the other value added
services were to be delivered to the rural citizens. As per the CSC guidelines
approved (September 2006) the State Designated Agency (SDA) i.e. Centre
for e-Governance was responsible for the overall management and monitoring
of the scheme. SDA was to select Service Center Agencies (SCAs) from the
private sector, through a bid process, for a period of 5 years (one year for
rollout and four years for operation) on ‘Public -Private Partnership (PPP)'
model. Request for Proposal (RFP) for bids was to be finalized in consultation
with Infrastructure Leasing & Financial Services (IL&FS) consultants
appointed by DIT, GOI as National Level Service Agency (NLSA). The
NLSA was responsible for conceptualization, as also all operational and
implementation activities for the project. These centres were to be established
by Village Level Entrepreneur (VLE) recruited by the SCAs in a village each
selected among cluster of six revenue villages.

As per the RFP finalized by the NLSA, CSCs would generate 75 percent of
revenue from Business to Citizen (B2C) services and 25 percent revenue from
G2C services. As per the Master Service Agreement (MSA) G2C services
were stated to be made available to CSCs as and when available.

The State selected four private companies as SCAs and MSA with seven
SCAs was signed (April 2008) to establish 17909 CSCs in seven zones (Agra,

56


Chapter 2: Performance Audit

Bareilly, Faizabad, Kanpur, Lucknow, Moradabad and Varanasi) of the State.
As per Government Order (GO) (May 2008) 25 per cent of the targeted CSCs
were to be established in four phases as detailed in Table 1.

The reason for non-establishment of the CSCs in the rural areas was
